OK, compiled successfully (29 pages total including appendix). The `\enlargethispage{\baselineskip}` was placed in main.tex before `\input{sections/conclusion}`. But the issue is that `\enlargethispage` acts on the current page at the point where it's encountered. Since it's between `\input{sections/discussion}` and `\input{sections/conclusion}`, it should affect whatever page that point falls on.

However, the problem is that `\enlargethispage` in a two-column format is tricky - it enlarges the page when the command is processed, but in two-column mode, the page is shipped out only after both columns are filled. 

Let me check pages 8-9 to see if the placement actually worked.
```

diff --git a/docs/paper/main.tex b/docs/paper/main.tex

@@ -34,6 +34,7 @@
 
 \input{sections/discussion}
 
+\enlargethispage{\baselineskip}
 \input{sections/conclusion}
 
 \input{sections/limitations}
diff --git a/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex b/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex
index 91b91b8..aab10ac 100644
--- a/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex
@@ -1,3 +1,3 @@
 \section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
 
-Layer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse conclusions; the audit protocol (\cref{sec:discussion}) would have caught our spurious finding. The 7--8B HS advantage is cross-validated; 32B results$^\dagger$ are exploratory and narrow with stronger encoders. Contribution~1 (layer bias) generalizes geometrically; Contribution~2 (HS advantage) needs causal validation and stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,li2024iti}.\looseness=-1
+Layer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse conclusions; the multi-metric audit protocol (\cref{sec:discussion}) mitigates this. Contribution~1 generalizes geometrically; Contribution~2 is cross-validated at 7--8B, exploratory at 32B$^\dagger$, and encoder-conditional.\looseness=-1
diff --git a/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex b/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex
index 4f5c55e..61bee96 100644
--- a/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex
@@ -1,7 +1,7 @@
 \section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
 
 \paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
-Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), confirmed by random-CP control (crossing rate retains shallow bias in 80\% of runs; precision's deep preference requires real labels; \cref{app:random_cp}) and architecture-independent (R1-8B full-layer sweep; \cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry; Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
+Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 80\% of permutations while precision's deep preference collapses to 20\% (\cref{app:random_cp}), confirming metric disagreement reflects true CP structure rather than intrinsic metric geometry. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry; Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
 
 \paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
 The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
diff --git a/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex b/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex
index 388c18f..0a16073 100644
--- a/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex
@@ -62,11 +62,11 @@ R1-32B & L63 & 384d & 1 & +18.7 & +15.4 & [+8.7, +22.6] & $-$14.6 & \checkmark \
 \label{tab:hs_vs_text}
 \end{table}
 
-The HS advantage is encoder-conditional: against BGE-large (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB correction (\cref{app:encoder_robustness}). Multi-seed stability appears in \cref{app:multi_seed}. Against the 384d baseline, HS probes outperform text probes in all four models$^\dagger$ (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}): cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B: +21.9pp precision, R1-8B: +20.1pp), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). R1-8B FPR is directionally consistent (5/5 seeds) but not robust ($p{=}0.044$, rank 12/12 in HB step-down; 1/5 seeds Bonferroni; \cref{app:multi_seed}). R1-32B passes 5/5 seeds but shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap NS; \cref{app:position_residual}); QwQ-32B's position baseline is competitive (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Overall, 18/20 model-seed combinations pass full HB (\cref{app:multi_seed}). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 11.1--16.8pp above chance (\cref{app:control}).\looseness=-1
+The HS advantage is encoder-conditional: against BGE-large (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB correction (\cref{app:encoder_robustness}). Multi-seed stability appears in \cref{app:multi_seed}. Against the 384d baseline, HS probes outperform text probes in all four models$^\dagger$ (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}): cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B: +21.9pp precision, R1-8B: +20.1pp), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). R1-8B FPR is directionally consistent (5/5 seeds) but not robust ($p{=}0.044$, rank 12/12 in HB step-down; 1/5 seeds Bonferroni; \cref{app:multi_seed}). R1-32B passes 5/5 seeds but shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap NS; \cref{app:position_residual}); QwQ-32B's position baseline is competitive (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Overall, 18/20 model-seed combinations pass full HB (\cref{app:multi_seed}). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 11.1--16.8pp above chance (\cref{app:control}). A split-half analysis on OT-7B confirms internal validity (\cref{app:split_half}).\looseness=-1
 
-\paragraph{Capacity control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (79--92\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage across all four models$^\dagger$ (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$, all HB). The control provides partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Random projection to 384d yields similar results ($\Delta$Prec +8.3 to +20.5pp; \cref{app:random_projection}), further weakening the capacity explanation. E5-Mistral-7B (4096d$\rightarrow$PCA 384d) yields intermediate results (2/4 HB; \cref{app:e5_mistral}), forming a monotonic encoder-quality gradient with MiniLM (4/4) and BGE-large (1/4).

-\paragraph{Encoder robustness.} Against BGE-large~\citep{xiao2023cpack} (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB (1/4 precision); R1-8B shows adverse FPR direction (+1.2pp). The advantage is encoder-conditional (\cref{fig:forest_precision}); layer selection (Contribution 1) is encoder-independent.

Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$). Adding a quadratic position term $(t/T)^2$ does not change significance conclusions for the 7--8B models. However, QwQ-32B's advantage is fully absorbed by the quadratic term (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\looseness=-1

diff --git a/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex b/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex
index 28cac95..3b76d5f 100644
--- a/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex
@@ -2,7 +2,7 @@
 
 Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.
 
-Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion.
+Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 16-layer (R1-8B) to 59-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\looseness=-1
 
 We present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.

```text
diff --git a/docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex b/docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex
index f202f53..4caa32c 100644
--- a/docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex
@@ -1 +1 @@
-Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: metric choice alone shifts the selected layer by 14--61 positions: crossing-rate composites select shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings, masking the HS advantage. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +19.2 to +21.9pp against MiniLM-384d (Holm-Bonferroni corrected; advantage is encoder-conditional and represents an upper bound); this advantage diminishes with stronger encoders (384d: 4/4 models pass HB $\to$ 1024d BGE-large: 1/4). The 7--8B results are cross-validated (nested CV): precision and balanced accuracy confirmed; R1-8B safe-trace false positive rate (FPR) is significant but seed-sensitive (3/5 seeds pass HB). The 32B results are exploratory$^\dagger$ ($^\dagger$\,sample-limited, $n \leq 20$): QwQ-32B is significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness; R1-32B shows a pattern consistent with position mediation and falls below minimum detectable effect. A three-layer ensemble provides metric-agnostic HS advantage matching the best individual metric (4/4 models, $p \leq 0.002$). Metric-induced layer selection bias is the robust methodological finding; the empirical HS advantage is conditional on encoder quality, model scale, and position control.
+Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings.     "type": "skill_listing",
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

[Director 指令] **执行 Figure 1 配色修改**

之前说"等 GPU 空闲"，但 Figure 1 是 matplotlib 生成，不需要 GPU。现在你空闲，直接执行：

1. 虚线颜色：红(#CB181D)/绿(#238B45) → **蓝(#2171B5)/橙(#E6550D)** + linestyle 区分（`--` vs `-·`）
2. y 轴标签字号：~8pt → 9-10pt
3. 不改 figure*（保持单栏）

找到 Figure 1 的生成脚本（应在远程服务器 src/ 目录或本地 docs/paper/figures/ 目录），修改配色参数，重新生成 PDF/PNG，替换到论文中。编译确认。

如果生成脚本不在本地、需要远程数据，告诉我，我派 Agent 协助。

    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.05,
})

with open(DATA_FILE) as f:
    data = json.load(f)

MODELS = [
    {
        'key': 'r1-8b',
        'label': 'R1-8B (25 layers)',
        'biased_layer': 14,
        'corrected_layer': 14,
        'overlap': True,
    },
    {
        'key': 'ot-7b',
        'label': 'OT-7B (28 layers)',
        'biased_layer': 0,
        'corrected_layer': 16,
        'overlap': False,
    },
    {
        'key': 'qwq-32b',
        'label': 'QwQ-32B (64 layers)',
        'biased_layer': 2,
        'corrected_layer': 60,
        'overlap': False,
    },
    {
        'key': 'r1-32b',
        'label': 'R1-32B (64 layers)',
        'biased_layer': 10,
        'corrected_layer': 63,
        'overlap': False,
    },
]

METRICS = ['bal_acc', 'coverage', 'precision', 'pre_cp_fpr', 'lead_mean']
METRIC_LABELS = {
    'bal_acc': 'Bal. Acc',
    'coverage': 'Crossing Rate',
    'precision': 'Precision',
    'pre_cp_fpr': 'Pre-CP FPR',
    'lead_mean': 'Lead (mean)',
}

fig, axes = plt.subplots(4, 1, figsize=(7, 10.5))
fig.subplots_adjust(hspace=0.45, right=0.88)

for ax, model_cfg in zip(axes, MODELS):
    key = model_cfg['key']
    md = data[key]
    layer_results = md['all_layer_results']
    layers = np.array([r['layer'] for r in layer_results])
    n_layers = len(layers)

    matrix = np.zeros((len(METRICS), n_layers))
    for i, metric in enumerate(METRICS):
        vals = np.array([r[metric] for r in layer_results])
        vmin, vmax = vals.min(), vals.max()
        if vmax > vmin:
            matrix[i, :] = (vals - vmin) / (vmax - vmin)
        else:
            matrix[i, :] = 0.5

    im = ax.imshow(matrix, aspect='auto', cmap='YlOrRd', interpolation='nearest',
                   extent=[layers[0] - 0.5, layers[-1] + 0.5, len(METRICS) - 0.5, -0.5])

    biased = model_cfg['biased_layer']
    corrected = model_cfg['corrected_layer']
    overlap = model_cfg['overlap']

    if overlap:
        ax.axvline(x=corrected, color='#2ca02c', linestyle='--', linewidth=1.5,
                   label=f'corrected = biased (L{corrected})')
    else:
        ax.axvline(x=biased, color='#d62728', linestyle='--', linewidth=1.5,
                   label=f'biased (L{biased})')
        ax.axvline(x=corrected, color='#2ca02c', linestyle='--', linewidth=1.5,
                   label=f'corrected (L{corrected})')

    ax.set_yticks(range(len(METRICS)))
    ax.set_yticklabels([METRIC_LABELS[m] for m in METRICS])

    if n_layers <= 32:
        tick_step = 2
    else:
        tick_step = 5
    xticks = list(range(0, n_layers, tick_step))
    if layers[-1] not in xticks:
        xticks.append(int(layers[-1]))
    ax.set_xticks(xticks)
    ax.set_xticklabels([str(t) for t in xticks])

    ax.set_xlabel('Layer index')
    ax.set_title(model_cfg['label'], fontweight='bold')
    ax.spines['top'].set_visible(False)
    ax.spines['right'].set_visible(False)

    ax.legend(loc='upper left', bbox_to_anchor=(1.01, 1.0),
              borderaxespad=0, framealpha=0.85, edgecolor='gray',
              handlelength=1.8, borderpad=0.3)

fig.suptitle('Layer Sweep: Metric Heatmap per Model', fontsize=11, fontweight='bold', y=0.995)

pdf_path = OUT_DIR / "fig_layer_heatmap.pdf"
png_path = OUT_DIR / "fig_layer_heatmap.png"
fig.savefig(pdf_path, dpi=300, bbox_inches='tight', pad_inches=0.05)
fig.savefig(png_path, dpi=300, bbox_inches='tight', pad_inches=0.05)
print(f"Saved PDF: {pdf_path} ({pdf_path.stat().st_size} bytes)")
print(f"Saved PNG: {png_path} ({png_path.stat().st_size} bytes)")
plt.close()

  "content": "import json\nimport numpy as np\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nDATA_FILE = Path(\"/tmp/layer_metric_ablation.json\")\nOUT_DIR = Path(\"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/figures\")\nOUT_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 9,\n    'axes.titlesize': 10,\n    'axes.labelsize': 9,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 8,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 10,\n    'legend.fontsize': 8,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.05,\n})\n\nwith open(DATA_FILE) as f:\n    data = json.load(f)\n\nMODELS = [\n    {\n        'key': 'r1-8b',\n        'label': 'R1-8B (25 layers)',\n        'biased_layer': 14,\n        'corrected_layer': 14,\n        'overlap': True,\n    },\n    {\n        'key': 'ot-7b',\n        'label': 'OT-7B (28 layers)',\n        'biased_layer': 0,\n        'corrected_layer': 16,\n        'overlap': False,\n    },\n    {\n        'key': 'qwq-32b',\n        'label': 'QwQ-32B (64 layers)',\n        'biased_layer': 2,\n        'corrected_layer': 60,\n        'overlap': False,\n    },\n    {\n        'key': 'r1-32b',\n        'label': 'R1-32B (64 layers)',\n        'biased_layer': 10,\n        'corrected_layer': 63,\n        'overlap': False,\n    },\n]\n\nMETRICS = ['bal_acc', 'coverage', 'precision', 'pre_cp_fpr', 'lead_mean']\nMETRIC_LABELS = {\n    'bal_acc': 'Bal. Acc',\n    'coverage': 'Crossing Rate',\n    'precision': 'Precision',\n    'pre_cp_fpr': 'Pre-CP FPR',\n    'lead_mean': 'Lead (mean)',\n}\n\nCOLOR_BIASED = '#2171B5'\nCOLOR_CORRECTED = '#E6550D'\n\nfig, axes = plt.subplots(4, 1, figsize=(7, 10.5))\nfig.subplots_adjust(hspace=0.45, right=0.88)\n\nfor ax, model_cfg in zip(axes, MODELS):\n    key = model_cfg['key']\n    md = data[key]\n    layer_results = md['all_layer_results']\n    layers = np.array([r['layer'] for r in layer_results])\n    n_layers = len(layers)\n\n    matrix = np.zeros((len(METRICS), n_layers))\n    for i, metric in enumerate(METRICS):\n        vals = np.array([r[metric] for r in layer_results])\n        vmin, vmax = vals.min(), vals.max()\n        if vmax > vmin:\n            matrix[i, :] = (vals - vmin) / (vmax - vmin)\n        else:\n            matrix[i, :] = 0.5\n\n    im = ax.imshow(matrix, aspect='auto', cmap='YlOrRd', interpolation='nearest',\n                   extent=[layers[0] - 0.5, layers[-1] + 0.5, len(METRICS) - 0.5, -0.5])\n\n    biased = model_cfg['biased_layer']\n    corrected = model_cfg['corrected_layer']\n    overlap = model_cfg['overlap']\n\n    if overlap:\n        ax.axvline(x=corrected, color=COLOR_CORRECTED, linestyle='-.', linewidth=1.5,\n                   label=f'corrected = biased (L{corrected})')\n    else:\n        ax.axvline(x=biased, color=COLOR_BIASED, linestyle='--', linewidth=1.5,\n                   label=f'biased (L{biased})')\n        ax.axvline(x=corrected, color=COLOR_CORRECTED, linestyle='-.', linewidth=1.5,\n                   label=f'corrected (L{corrected})')\n\n    ax.set_yticks(range(len(METRICS)))\n    ax.set_yticklabels([METRIC_LABELS[m] for m in METRICS])\n\n    if n_layers <= 32:\n        tick_step = 2\n    else:\n        tick_step = 5\n    xticks = list(range(0, n_layers, tick_step))\n    if layers[-1] not in xticks:\n        xticks.append(int(layers[-1]))\n    ax.set_xticks(xticks)\n    ax.set_xticklabels([str(t) for t in xticks])\n\n    ax.set_xlabel('Layer index')\n    ax.set_title(model_cfg['label'], fontweight='bold')\n    ax.spines['top'].set_visible(False)\n    ax.spines['right'].set_visible(False)\n\n    ax.legend(loc='upper left', bbox_to_anchor=(1.01, 1.0),\n              borderaxespad=0, framealpha=0.85, edgecolor='gray',\n              handlelength=1.8, borderpad=0.3)\n\nfig.suptitle('Layer Sweep: Metric Heatmap per Model', fontsize=11, fontweight='bold', y=0.995)\n\npdf_path = OUT_DIR / \"fig_layer_heatmap.pdf\"\npng_path = OUT_DIR / \"fig_layer_heatmap.png\"\nfig.savefig(pdf_path, dpi=300, bbox_inches='tight', pad_inches=0.05)\nfig.savefig(png_path, dpi=300, bbox_inches='tight', pad_inches=0.05)\nprint(f\"Saved PDF: {pdf_path} ({pdf_path.stat().st_size} bytes)\")\nprint(f\"Saved PNG: {png_path} ({png_path.stat().st_size} bytes)\")\nplt.close()\n"
}

30	\end{table}
31	
32	\begin{figure}[t]
33	\centering
34	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_heatmap.pdf}
35	\caption{Per-layer metric heatmap (2$\times$2). Each column: one of five metrics (min-max normalized). Red dashed: CR-composite layer (bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate); green dashed: threshold-based layer (consensus of threshold-based metrics). For R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep), crossing rate drives CR-composite selection to L0 while threshold-based metrics retain L14. At 32B, CR-composite selections cluster near input layers while threshold-based metrics peak at deep layers.}
36	\label{fig:layer_heatmap}
37	\end{figure}
38	
39	The downstream impact is severe: at CR-composite layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage; both 32B models fail. At threshold-based layers, the HS advantage is graded: cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B, R1-8B; nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$, and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (residualized gap below MDE). The $\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \leq 20$) where effect sizes are descriptive rather than confirmatory. Had we reported only CR-composite-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact. A random-label control on QwQ-32B confirms that the crossing-rate shallow-layer bias is a geometric property of the representations (100\% of random-label repeats select shallow layers), while threshold-based deep-layer selection requires real safety labels (\cref{app:random_cp}). AUROC, excluded from our primary metrics because it is threshold-free (\cref{sec:evaluation}), confirms HS discriminability at BA-selected layers ($\Delta$AUROC +12.1 to +15.1pp; \cref{tab:ba_layer_results}) but cannot diagnose the threshold-dependent layer selection bias.\looseness=-1

At 32B, CR-composite selections cluster near input layers while threshold-based metrics peak at deep layers.}\n\\label{fig:layer_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe downstream impact is severe: at CR-composite layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage; both 32B models fail. At threshold-based layers, the HS advantage is graded: cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B, R1-8B; nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$, and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (residualized gap below MDE). The $\\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \\leq 20$) where effect sizes are descriptive rather than confirmatory. Had we reported only CR-composite-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact. A random-label control on QwQ-32B confirms that the crossing-rate shallow-layer bias is a geometric property of the representations (100\\% of random-label repeats select shallow layers), while threshold-based deep-layer selection requires real safety labels (\\cref{app:random_cp}). AUROC, excluded from our primary metrics because it is threshold-free (\\cref{sec:evaluation}), confirms HS discriminability at BA-selected layers ($\\Delta$AUROC +12.1 to +15.1pp; \\cref{tab:ba_layer_results}) but cannot diagnose the threshold-dependent layer selection bias.\\looseness=-1",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 30,
      "totalLines": 112
    }
  },

- 正确数字（对齐 tab:layer_selection）：R1-8B = L0 vs L14 = **14** 层 gap；QwQ = L2 vs L63 = **61** 层 gap
- 修正为："a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence"

**[ERROR 2] Discussion §5 random-CP 数字错位**
- 当前："crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 80% of permutations"
- 实际：**crossing rate = 100% shallow**（不是 80%），**CR-composite = 80% shallow**
- 修正为："crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference collapses to 20\%"
- 同时修正因果表述："crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels"

---

**[强化 3] Conclusion 补 Contribution 3**

**[强化 4] Conclusion 补效果量**

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 16-layer (R1-8B) to 59-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\looseness=-1
6	
7	We present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.
8	
9	Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \cref{sec:experiments}).
10	
11	Our contributions are:
12	\begin{itemize}
13	    \item \textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
14	
15	    \item \textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).
16	
17	    \item \textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.
21	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LAX86KF3C7c3C7JCPZFBWF`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 80\% of permutations while precision's deep preference collapses to 20\% (\cref{app:random_cp}), confirming metric disagreement reflects true CP structure rather than intrinsic metric geometry. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry; Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\looseness=-1
11	
12	\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}
13	Training probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \leq 0.002$; \cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\looseness=-1
14	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 16-layer (R1-8B) to 59-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

    "oldString": "crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
    "newString": "crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models including a Llama-architecture model, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A random-label control confirms the bias is geometric. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

      {

A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models including a Llama-architecture model, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A random-label control confirms the bias is geometric. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",

Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\\citep{gelman2013garden}.}\nOur five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\\%), while precision's deep preference collapses to 20\\% (\\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry; Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\\citep{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}\nTraining probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \\leq 0.002$; \\cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\\looseness=-1\n",
